ParlaMint-DK_20201020130539	The debate on the report was opened. I do not see that Mrs Annette Lind is here, and that is why it must be Mr Lind. Christoffer Aagaard Melson, whom I ask to take the floor.
ParlaMint-DK_20201020130617	Thank you for the floor and thank you to the Minister for Foreign Affairs for the statement. After all, 2020 has been a very special year because of the corona epidemic and, as the report shows, the epidemic has also greatly influenced the Arctic because of its long distances and the often worse opportunities for dealing with the pandemic locally. The situation has also meant that cooperation within the Arctic Council and political cooperation within the Kingdom community has been very limited. In a year when the new Arctic strategy is being drawn up, it is, of course, an extra pity. We look forward very much to the fact that, hopefully, cooperation will soon be restored to normal. Despite the corona epidemic, the events this year have again confirmed that, in addition to forming the framework of the Kingdom, the Arctic is also of crucial foreign policy importance to the Kingdom. The importance of the Arctic to Denmark has become much more evident in recent years. As you know, the increased focus in Danish media started seriously in August 2019 when it emerged that President Trump was considering buying Greenland. Then followed the announcement that the United States would open a consulate in Greenland, and a new cooperation agreement between Greenland and the United States, which followed an amount of 83 million. kr. with, followed the troop in the spring. The great focus on Greenland and the kingdom community was finally rounded off with the American Foreign Minister's visit to Copenhagen on 22 July. Many Danes have probably also seen Christina Rosendahl's film in recent months • Our husband in America. The film should, in my view, be a permanent curriculum in primary school, as it gives a very good understanding of how crucial Greenland in particular, but also the Faroe Islands are for Denmark's history and foreign policy situation to this day. The film is about the Danish ambassador to Washington during World War II, Henrik Kauffmann. The film illustrates in an excellent way how crucial the question of Greenland was for Denmark's security policy situation during and after World War II. Had Henrik Kauffmann not without a mandate allied with the Americans and made an agreement that they could set up bases in Greenland in defence against Germany first and later the Soviet Union, there was no doubt that Greenland today in some form had been part of the United States. History also shows that Greenland's military strategic importance was crucial for the Americans to work after World War II to make Denmark one of the founding countries of the United Nations and later become part of NATO. Although the world is different today, the military strategic importance of the Kingdom Community is still one of the main reasons why the United States is prioritising very close cooperation with Denmark. And history shows how crucial alliances and an international legal order are for population small countries such as Greenland, the Faroe Islands and Denmark. Therefore, it is also very gratifying that the focus is precisely on the value of NATO as the foundation for our security and the value of an international legal order, and cooperation in the Arctic Council in particular has a prominent role in the statement. The increased American focus on Greenland and also on the Faroe Islands, as we see in these years, is linked to an increased Russian but also Chinese activity in the Arctic. It is clear from the statement that in recent years Russia has armed heavily military forces in the Arctic, which is quite understandable to the Americans, who, among other things, are concerned. fear that the new Russian military installations can be used to hit Thulebasen. The possibility of new shipping routes due to global warming is probably also a factor in the increased interest in the Arctic. In the Left, we believe that the interests of the great powers should be a wakeup call for all of us in the Kingdom community. This means that we need to give priority to increased dialogue and coordination within the Kingdom community. Today, fortunately, there is a more equal relationship between Denmark, the Faroe Islands and Greenland than there was during the Second World War and during the Cold War. We must also have a debate in the Commonwealth about where the lines of security policy are going. If, for example, we do not know what to do. agree on what is critical infrastructure, it will be harder for other countries to play us off against each other; we also look forward to contributing to that debate in the coming year. The increased activity in the Arctic also makes increased demands on the kingdom, compared to the fact that we can keep track of what is going on, for example. along the east coast of Greenland and in relation to the ability to carry out emergency tasks. These are tasks to which we will also have to give financial priority in the coming years before that, of course, an Arctic package needs to be negotiated with the Minister for Defence, and this is also a debate that we look forward to contributing constructively to. We do not think that, for example, there is a need for a common policy on the environment. better Danish surveillance of Greenland's east coast, a stronger preparedness or a better ability to enforce the sovereignty of the kingdom is in contrast to our common ambition for low-voltage. We believe that the development of recent years has shown that other countries will fill a vacuum if there is a security policy vacuum in the Arctic, and this is a vacuum that we should be able to fill in order to maintain low-voltage. Similarly, we believe that we should be actively involved in the issue of future border migration in the Arctic. If we do not respond We must not only pay attention to the physical threats; we must also be wary of the developments in which several countries in these years are withdrawing from international agreements or increasingly ignoring international law, respect for land borders and international agreements. As a small country with an export-oriented economy, fine-meshed, rule-based international cooperation is crucial for both our security and our economy. It is a world order that we must make every effort to strengthen and develop. As far as the Arctic is concerned, we must therefore fight for cooperation in the Arctic Council, which has helped to ensure, fortunately, that the situation in the Arctic remains relatively peaceful. Our location as an Arctic coastal state gives us a unique opportunity to shape the development on a geographically huge part of our globe. Of course, we must do everything we can to make use of that opportunity. The great influence offers great opportunities, but also great commitments to ensure the best possible outcome for the many different discussions on fisheries, the exploitation of raw materials, sustainable economy, economic development, whaling, seal-catching, transport and climate. The areas in which we believe in particular that we as a country should give priority are issues relating to the environment and climate. Ecosystems in the Arctic are very sensitive, which is why it is important to have a readiness to respond if, for example, there is a need for a response. oil spills from ships. The description of the accelerating climate change in the Arctic is hard reading, among other things. is the thickest and oldest ice cream reduced by 90%. This illustrates why it is important that we take the lead in the fight against climate change and engage in the fight for the climate in international fora, where we have the opportunity to exert our influence. The alarming increase in temperature is also one of the reasons why we in the Left believe that we should give priority to research in the Arctic. We have an opportunity and a commitment to help the world understand the climate change we see in the Arctic, thereby opening their eyes to the consequences of climate change. As an end to that priority, one of our wishes in the negotiations on the research reserve is therefore that funds should also be given priority to a new research ship which can contribute to this research, and I look forward to hearing the Government's position on prioritising such a research vessel in the research negotiations. Finally, of course, there must also be a little bit of rice for the Foreign Minister: I think there is a lack of focus on how we can create the most optimal framework for sustainable development in the region, in the statement. It is about breaking down barriers that hamper development. It is about creating understanding and respect for local people's way of life and needs. We believe that one of the most positive developments in recent year was the opening up of the American Foreign Minister to increased free trade with the Faroe Islands. The opening up of new markets to the countries of the Kingdom Community is essential to ensure the desired economic development of the region, but it is also crucial in terms of security policy, so that all countries in the Kingdom community become less dependent on pressure from individual countries by having more export markets open to them. It should be a key priority for Denmark to help Greenland and the Faroe Islands also open new export markets. It is very good that the Americans spend a little more money searching for opportunities to attract American investment in Greenland, but it should be perfectly natural for Denmark to work in cooperation with Greenland and the Faroe Islands to attract possible investments. The Government's efforts to make all three parts of the Kingdom community richer in the future by increasing investment and trade across the Kingdom community are unfortunately also absent from the statement. I hope that we can have a good debate on those issues too today. I look forward to the continued debate. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020143111	Thank you. There is an increasing rivalry in the Arctic, and there is a systematic build-up on the part of both Russia and the United States. Denmark has also increased its military presence. The fight for raw materials and opportunities for profit will be tough. Things change so quickly that the current Danish strategy is outdated, and therefore it is important that we get a new strategy for the Arctic in the Kingdom Community. Our first meeting with colleagues from Greenland and the Faroe Islands was very fruitful. On an equal footing, we discussed at a major conference of the network six areas that are important to us all: that it is a peaceful and secure Arctic; that there is trustful and regional and international cooperation; that people in the Arctic should be at the centre; that there should be sustainable economic development; that protection of nature and environment and climate is important; that new knowledge and research on the Arctic should be strengthened. A genuinely fruitful and equal common strategy was envisaged between the three parts of the Kingdom Community for parliamentarians, ministers and civil society, but that is not what it is meant to be. When I asked the Foreign Minister the other day when the six topics I mentioned were to be drilled out and put on the agenda for six sub-conferences between Members of Parliament and others in the Kingdom Community, I was told that this was not meant to happen. The rest of the work is to be done in cooperation between the three ministers and their officials, and they are the ones who finally hit the nail. And it's the ministers' that's disappointing. I thought we were now facing a new break from the Secretary of State. A new government, a new era and a new influence. But no, everything's old. Perhaps we could learn lessons from, for example. Parliament's work on a new strategy for developing cooperation in the Third World. Here the rapporteurs meet, who have set in motion a longer process to renew the current strategy, and civil society organisations offer proposals. It is the majority of the Folketing that decides what the final strategy should look like. I would like the Minister for Foreign Affairs to learn from his colleague, the Minister for Development. After all, there are only a few steps from one office to another, so perhaps experience could be exchanged about which process is perhaps the most profitable and perhaps the most democratic. For Enhedslisten there are two guiding principles for activity in the Arctic. The first is that all activities must take account of the people living in the Arctic. There are immensely many economic interests that are immensely numerous military and security interests in the Arctic, but from first to last it must always be a question of how the people living in the Arctic will have a good and sustainable life. We must respect the right to self-determination and the concern for indigenous peoples. The second principle is that all activities we launch in the Arctic must be based on sustainable principles, taking optimal account of the very sensitive nature facing change due to climate change. The Arctic is more exposed to climate change than the rest of the world, because the temperature increases here are 2-3 times as large. It is important that we maintain the Ilulissat agreement in 2008 that the Arctic should be a low-voltage military area and that all disagreements about the Arctic should be resolved through negotiation on the basis of international international law. Without Denmark's cooperation with Greenland and the Faroe Islands in the Kingdom Community, Denmark would have no particular influence in the Arctic. Therefore, it is important that we give Greenland and the Faroe Islands optimal influence in the Arctic Council and in other forums and negotiations on the Arctic. The Foreign Minister calls this new great power rivalry, which is taking place in the Arctic, a new dynamic in the Arctic. We have two different views on the word dynamic, I can hear. The Foreign Minister welcomes the United States' second fleet in the area and promises increased Danish military presence, knowing that, together with the Russians' activities, it is the start of an armament spiral, which is the opposite of what is stated in the Ilulissat Agreement. I don't know how long he's going to call the United States the Kingdom's closest ally. It is a pretty ally that we have at the moment, which denounces one international agreement after another and which, moreover, left up to 30 military bases in Greenland without cleaning up after it. The foundation for our security is not to develop an armament spiral. Our security lies in international disarmament agreements and stronger efforts to ensure that all Arctic states comply with the principles of the Ilulissat Declaration. Our security lies in a Nordic alternative to the rivalry of the great powers in the Arctic. I could well imagine that the wise Foreign Ministers in the North sat down and said: how can we become an alternative? We are just as large and as strong as the others when it comes to economics and insight into what is happening in the Arctic, research capacity, etc. It is important that Denmark takes an active part in research on Arctic themes, not least when it comes to how to ensure sustainable living opportunities for the people and how to ensure optimum consideration of nature and not least of the seas, and how to ensure environmentally sound transport in the environmentally sensitive area. In this context, it is important that we build a new research ship to replace the old Dana. It is necessary to be very ambitious when building the ship and why not build a joint research capacity in closer cooperation with the other Nordic countries? I would like to see a large international research centre in Greenland, where the Arctic states share their knowledge of the situation and how we together ensure nature, environment and climate, and not in imperialist competition. People and nature are the first two priorities when we have to do activities in the Arctic, which I mentioned before. We need tough and binding international agreements on the extraction of raw materials in the future exposed land and sea areas. There must be an international ban on the extraction of oil and environmentally harmful raw materials in the Arctic. It will have incalculable consequences for nature if emissions occur in a climate in which pollution control will be virtually impossible. Long enough, there has been discussion and negotiation of a ban on the use and transport of heavy fuel, what is called heavy fuel oil in the Arctic. We need action now, Minister, we do not need to see any further delaying meetings. We have to see that this ban is being implemented. It has been a long time since that ban was implemented for Antarctica; the same has to happen up here in the north. The same applies to the fight against highly degradable substances, soot pollution and contamination with plastics and microplastics in the Arctic. All in all, the report contains many minor individual initiatives between countries and they are all steps in the right direction. But in view of the desire of companies and countries to make money, big money, in the Arctic and the rivalry and armaments of the great powers, things are going too slowly. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020144717	Thank you very much. After all, this is an extremely important debate, which I have also looked forward to. And I have actually been very much in agreement with many of the things that have been said in the past by the rapporteurs. Among other things: I could recognize myself very much in Mr. Martin Lidegaard's introduction of a desire for peaceful development, economic development and sustainable development in the Arctic. I fully agree with that. I think the rest of Parliament will agree with that. I'm not going to make an attempt to make such a full investigation about everything that's going to happen in the Arctic, because it doesn't allow time or my mental capacity or knowledge. However, I would like to make a few points of impact on some of the things that I think are important and that I hope the government will take into account in its work. Generally speaking, there should be the same principles for the development of society in the Arctic as there are in the rest of the Commonwealth and in the rest of the Western world: we believe in openness, transparency, market economy, free trade, international exchange, and of course also a great focus on sustainable development. All of this must be done within the framework of the Kingdom community, and there is nothing wrong with that either. The Kingdom Community is an enormously flexible structure and it allows you to have plenty of international exchange, trade, etc., as much as you would like, and much more of that can be done. The kingdom community must be constantly developed and strengthened, and it should fill much more in the work of Danish governments. It is not a criticism of the current government, it is just such a general criticism of all the governments that have been in recent Danish history: the kingdom community and the development of the Arctic should fill more. This should not be a small area under the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. It should be its own ministry, and it should be a very large focus area. The Kingdom Community is not a foreign policy, it is the direct task of the Danish government and the Folketing, since the Folketing is the parliament of the Kingdom and not a Danish parliament. It is a task that is becoming more and more important because there are more and more forces that are pushing both the Arctic and the Commonwealth, and the government could make a big difference if it put itself in a position to strengthen cooperation on business development, tourism, export, education, research and a wide range of other areas where we could get much more out of the synergies between our three countries. Here in the Folketing it is also very obvious, I think, that we take our own responsibility on us and make greater efforts to work together within the Kingdom community by simply grabbing the North Atlantic members who sit directly down here and who are available to us every day and with whom we could have closer cooperation. In fact, I think that a very serious effort is being made by the North Atlantic members and that we really get a lot of good input from them sitting here in the Folketing and can tell us about the cases where the shoe prints and where there is a need for legislation or other initiatives to be made in a way that is more appropriate for the Faroe Islands and Greenland. And I think we have a lot of that to do, but we could also get more out of it. I have long advocated that I think the North Atlantic members should have the opportunity to join, for example, the defence settlement on an equal footing with other members of the Folketing. I once spoke to a member of a government who told me that it would be unnatural because the defence is a Danish matter. And it is a pity that there are still such misunderstandings that defence and police and foreign policy, currency and other matters are Danish affairs. Because it's not. These are common matters, and that is why we have a common parliament here in the Folketing. It is just as strange and wrong to exclude Greenlandic and Faroese members of parliament from joining, for example, in the defence settlement, as if we told the members of parliament who are elected on Bornholm that they cannot be part of the defence settlement because it is a Zealand matter. It is actually the same, and it is a very strange way to put it up, that is to say that you say: 'Well, it is Zealand that pays; it is very much my own constituency out in Gentofte and in Lyngby-Taarbæk that pays the bill, and Bornholm is a deficit business that receives large subsidies from the state, and therefore they cannot be part of a defence settlement. I think perhaps that, for example, the Minister for Foreign Affairs would think that this was a rather unfair way of doing things. But it is actually the same thing that is said to the Faroese and Greenlandic members of the Folketing: that they cannot join because they do not pay the bill. And then they are excluded from influence in an area that has enormous importance both in the Faroe Islands and in Greenland. And I think that is wrong, and it is very obvious that if we are serious about the Kingdom community, we are also taking it on us here in the Folketing. In relation to the great powers, which take up a lot, I, like everyone else, was very shocked to wake up one morning to the fact that the American President had plans to buy Greenland. It is a totally anachronistic attitude that one could have 100 years ago, when one really sold the territories we sold, for example. The Danish West Indies to the United States just over 100 years ago; they could do so then, but they no longer do so, and it is completely out of step with all the values that we acknowledge today, that it was only in this way that a territory could be traded over the heads of the people who lived in that area and who had no wish for it. It was quite incredibly toned down and an extreme revelation of how little the American President knows about Greenland first of all about the conditions up there, but of course also in relation to our kingdom community. It has, of course, been a little awakening, and I think that we need to deal even more with the growing interest of various major powers, including Russia and China. I do not think we should be naive; I think we should give them space. It would be very, very strange to say to Chinese, American and Russian alike that they are not allowed to trade in Greenland. It would also be extremely anachronistic; then we would be back in colonial times when there were such monopolies of a commercial nature, which were the only ones that had to act in North Atlantic regions, and that would also be completely insane. So, of course, there must be the openness, transparency and access, as there is to Denmark too, of course, that must also apply in the North Atlantic. But I also think that we should sometimes set some counter-conditions. For example, it is now aired from the Russian side that they want a consul in Greenland. And that's very reliable and an excellent idea. I just think one can ask oneself the question: should we also have a consul in Arctic Russia, for example.? And there I would like to hear the government: when there are these opportunities, that is, when there are great powers coming and wanting something, what do we need to get back? Because this is an obvious situation when it comes to thinking: we certainly have some wishes for those we would like to see met. And there's always a bad trading position just to give something without getting anything back. I therefore believe that we should not just be inclined to say yes to anything, but that we should also set counter-conditions, such as those we have in mind. that the openness that Russia is now asking for is being reciprocated, so that we also have a knowledge of what is happening on the other side of the North Pole, where we also want to know what is going on. It is a bit worrying what Russia is doing in relation to the arms build-up that is taking place in the Arctic, and I certainly think there is reason to keep an eye on that. Nor do I share the naivety that I think some rapporteurs have expressed, that is to say we should just rust down while others rust up. Again: there has to be reciprocity in the way we act, and unilateral disarmament will certainly be very, very stupid and also difficult to reconcile with the need to maintain confidence in being a credible ally, ensuring that the territory for which we are responsible is protected and not just something that lies and that you can just come and take if you like. I don't think anyone has any interest in that. Therefore, the Danish defence must be able to enforce sovereignty in the areas that are Danish, and it is of course important that it remains at a level that is high enough for us to be able to carry out that task. I think there are major tasks ahead of us. I really hope that the government will give priority to this area. I think we could have done that in the past too, but now the government has the opportunity to do so, and I hope that we will do so and put an ambitious strategy, put a lot of resources into developing solutions that can strengthen the Kingdom community, and always based on the fact that it should benefit all three countries. The only way the kingdom community can survive is if both Denmark, Greenland and the Faroe Islands constantly have the advantages of being in it, and I hope the government will take great care to do so, and that the government will bear in mind that the government is not a Danish government but also a government for Greenland and the Faroe Islands.

Following on from the interest of the great powers in the Arctic, we look forward to the necessary debate and involvement of, among others, Greenland's business community, NGOs and political parties about the screening mechanism, which is also to be discussed here in the Folketing, in relation to Denmark. This is important in relation to the necessary foreign investment, which is so important for Greenland. On the part of the IA, we believe that we should be critical of, for example: Chinese investment in critical infrastructure so that we neither get into a debt trap nor provide access to vulnerable information about our population and society. At the same time, we also recognise that more and more areas are categorized as security policy, thereby challenging our self-determination in the areas that have been repatriated. It will put our cooperation to the test, and it will require that the close and good dialogue, which I have the impression there are these years, will be strengthened even more in the years to come. For IA, it is important to emphasise that a right of veto on the Danish side must not be abused. We would really like to ensure cooperation and ensure that Greenland's own role in our own country is not undermined. The government has for many years supported UArctic, which is a collaboration between universities in the Arctic. UArctic's work is important for Greenlandic students who have the opportunity to enter an exchange in the Arctic. This allows international teachers from the Arctic to come to Greenland and teach young Greenlanders and give them some new knowledge. This helps to create a rich international education environment, and that is why we would also call on the government to continue that grant of 3 million. kr., which up to now has been given to UArctic. This year we also know that research funds are being negotiated, and in reality it is really a pity that these critical voices are coming in, among other things. from Denmark, which is annoying about other countries' increasing research activity in Greenland, without our own efforts to ensure that we from Denmark also ensure increased research activity in Greenland. We need to change that, because if we want to be an Arctic power, it is also important that we have a much greater commitment to research, including greater involvement of the more and more Greenlandic researchers coming and joint projects between Greenland and Denmark. The government has also contributed to the establishment of an Arctic research hub at the University of Greenland, and it is a very good start that this initiative has been taken, but in reality it should be anchored to a much greater extent at home in Greenland. Last year we also talked about the establishment of the Greenland International Institute for Arctic Studies, which should focus on foreign policy and security policy, at the University of Greenland. We still believe that it is highly topical to focus on both a knowledge build-up in the field of foreign and security policy in Greenland, so that a more proactive and knowledge-based foreign policy can be conducted. A key motto for us at IA is: nothing about us without us. The message is quite simple: At all meetings and events about Greenland, Greenland should also sit with. Nothing about us without us. This is also true of the recent debate in Greenland on the municipality managers and villagers' access to welfare, which the undersigned took up during the opening debate. It is quite natural that we should listen to the people who live in the villages of Greenland and in the towns from which people move. My message today, as was also in the opening debate, is that strong settlements must be strengthened and settlements depopulated and with little basis for life need to be given more attention. An Arctic delegation has been set up, and all the parts of the empire have been taken into account to a much greater extent than has been done before. It's very important, and it also takes a lot to make this work. One thing is to coordinate economic, political and defence matters, but they must be coordinated by a kingdom which is itself a conglomerate. It is geographically, demographically and economically a combination of unimaginably unequal elements • the Kingdom of Denmark; it can hardly be more difficult to make it work. It is therefore incredibly important that we have a constant dialogue and understanding and knowledge so that each country has space, as well as that together we find solutions that we can stand together. I will deal with some things in the debate that has been going on. One is the one that has come from the Left. In several articles it has been said that Denmark must help put pressure on the Commission in Brussels so that Faroese goods have better access to the European market. This is something I have talked about many times myself, so I am happy with that proposal and I hope it will be received with open arms. That should be possible. After all, there are some wounds left from 2013-14, where the European Commission made some mistakes due to some erroneous information, some erroneous facts, boycotted the Faroe Islands completely for a whole year, and this created a very unfortunate situation in the Kingdom community, where Faroese ships could not access Danish ports for a whole year, and we had to change the entire export from one day to the next, and so we were driven into the Russian market. One should be able to use that as an argument for having something to make up for. Then, of course, as is shown in what has been written about this, it is on the Left that Russia is not a country that you would like to be very, very closely associated with economics. Russia is being very bully in these years, both in terms of military activity and, above all, in terms of constant disinformation, the constant attempts to destabilise democratic countries on the part of Russia day in and day out. It is not fortunate for the Faroe Islands to be very, very bound by exports to Russia, nor is it in Denmark's interest, nor is it in Europe's interest that we have such a very close connection with Russia, and therefore the government should be sensitive to the argument of opening up Faroese goods to the European market more easily. Then they'll do it too. Then I would like to say straight away that exporting to Russia sometimes sounds in the press in Denmark as if only the Faroe Islands are doing so. It's not like that. Denmark exports everything Russia wants to buy in Denmark and the whole of Europe exports everything Russia wants to buy in Europe. In fact, exports have been steadily increasing over the past many years. But in the Faroese case, of course, it is a little extreme because we are the only ones exporting food to Russia. I hope it will be possible for us to get an understanding of it in Brussels, so let us open it up. Where you are financially linked, you also become politically linked, so it will link the Faroe Islands closer to Europe, which I think would be in everyone's interest, but it just should not be done by force. As far as the Arctic strategy is concerned, I have a concrete proposal which is that the Faroe Islands should be involved as much as possible at the practical level. Both I and others have spoken of the need to replace the ships that enforce sovereignty in the North Atlantic. They are more than 30 years old, and it is old in this regard. Such ships do not have to be offered throughout Europe, they can be built on their own. There is no demand for it, because when it is military hardware, you do not have to put it in tender. There are two shipyards in Denmark, one of which is in the Faroe Islands, and if you get the Faroese shipyard involved, it will be a practical involvement which is extremely important for you to have such a sense of co-responsibility. There is also a radar that needs to be set up. Unfortunately, it needs to be set up. I am convinced of this, because we cannot allow all sorts of activities around the Faroe Islands which we are not aware of. It is incredibly important that you do not make the mistake that you made during the Cold War, namely that you just do it yourself, decide and then do it, but that you involve both Faroese authorities in the decision and involve the Faroese business community in the practical work, and here I am thinking of the whole It gives a sense of responsibility, a sense of being involved in this, which will be very valuable. With regard to the United States' interest in Greenland and partly also in the Faroe Islands, I would like to say that the American ambassador has been in the Faroe Islands several times and said that one would like to cooperate and that one would like to have everything possible up and running in cooperation with the Faroe Islands, and that is something that I very much welcome. I don't see what the problem is in it at all. During the Cold War, the only thing anyone saw was the United States on the Faroe Islands, soldiers, ships and radars; everything was military. You never saw anything civilian. It is far better to have a civil relationship with those who protect you, as it will give belonging a completely different dimension, which I think would benefit everyone. Many people are sceptical about the United States today because there is a President who undermines many pillars of democracy and who constantly attacks the electoral system, the judicial system, the press, and that is very worrying. But the system in the United States is bigger than the President, and that must always be kept in mind. It is completely futile to talk about China being better to cooperate with, because Trump is the way he is. China is a system that is anti-democratic, while the United States is a system that is democratic, but you have a president at the moment, which I understand you are a little worried about. After all, we have representations in Iceland, in Copenhagen, in Brussels, in London, in Beijing and in Moscow, but we have no one in Washington; there is no formal channel from the US authorities to the Faroese authorities, and that is why we as MPs are often contacted by the American Embassy, and they ask about that and that and about factual facts and how we feel about it and how we think about it and how we think about it. There is simply a lack of a channel that we have with both Russia and with China and with other countries. I think that is a mistake too. I see that time is running out, so I am not going to say so much more, but I hope that the concrete impetus for the Arctic strategy, the involvement of all parties at the specific level, both politically and practically, will be welcomed, because it is when it comes to practical matters, in other words when you go from word to action, that it means something. We should preferably not end up in the situation we had during the Cold War, where everything that was going on in the Faroe Islands felt like something foreign to the Faroese people, because they had not been involved in deciding it, had not been involved in doing so, because it was something that was agreed between Denmark and the United States, and then it was just there. It created very great resistance, and it was also what caused many conflicts between Denmark and the Faroe Islands during the Cold War, and it constantly created dissatisfaction in the form of protests and everything. That's what I had to say. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020162844	Thank you, President. First of all, thank you for a very good and constructive debate today, I think. I would also like to look forward here and mention the work on preparing Denmark, the Faroe Islands, Greenland and the Faroe Islands' new Arctic strategy, which should be in place at the beginning of 2021. It is important that the Kingdom acts on the basis of a common Arctic strategy, a common platform for our international Arctic work. The current strategy has demonstrated its strength by being a common frame of reference for the authorities, so that you can work with the Arctic across the whole kingdom. At the same time, it has ensured coherence and focus on the political approach, not least in relation to the Arctic Council, which is, of course, part of a key architecture in relation to the binding Arctic cooperation. Work on formulating a new strategy is in full swing throughout the Kingdom community. This is an inclusive process, I would also like to say to the Enhedslisten's rapporteur, where relevant actors are involved in the work of defining the visions and priorities that will shape the next 10 years of international engagement by Danish, Greenlandic and Faroese. In this context, I would like to highlight the parliamentarians' meeting which took place in June with the virtual participation of all three parliaments. It was a historic meeting in which the three parliaments for the first time jointly discussed developments in the Arctic and recorded the future strategy. I hope that the Members of Parliament will take on board this tradition. I can also hear that there is a great deal of appetite for this and I am glad that it looks as if the three parliaments will be able to discuss strategy again in the Arctic in a few weeks' time. It'll also be virtually due to the covid-19 situation we're in. In any case, I think it is important that we do so. I agree with the many comments made by the House today. As last year, the discussion has also focused very much on the significant developments taking place in the Arctic as a result of the new security policy dynamic, which challenges the Kingdom's and the other Arctic states' low-voltage objectives. We must remember that the developments we have seen in the Arctic in recent years are mainly due to climate change. They present quite specific challenges both globally and especially in the Arctic due to greater average temperature increases right here. At the same time, new economic opportunities are also being created, and they must of course benefit the Arctic population, as several people have also mentioned. There is no more doubt that the Arctic region is of ever increasing geopolitical importance to both the United States, Russia and China, and geopolitics is a basic condition, including in the Arctic today. Russia is making a significant expansion and consolidation of its military capacity along the Arctic coast. China is thinking long-term and showing increasing strategic interest in the Arctic. The United States has strengthened its civil commitment to Greenland and its military commitment to the Arctic and the North Atlantic. In the Arctic, Denmark, Greenland and the Faroe Islands must continue to pursue our common democratic values such as dialogue, trust, equality and rule-based cooperation; values that characterise our own societies. When international values come under pressure, there is, in fact, an even greater need for us to show a different way in our community. As an Arctic coastal state and a member of the Arctic Council, the Kingdom has an important role to play in influencing developments. The desire for peace and low-voltage will continue to be an essential priority for the Kingdom's Arctic policy. We simply cannot afford anything else. It is a personal priority for me that we create an up-to-date and inclusive kingdom community based on equal value, on respect, trust and mutual involvement, and here the new Arctic Strategy is an extremely important step. In this context, we must not forget that the Arctic region is much more than a security policy. After all, our objective remains to create a prosperous and sustainable future for the Arctic. We must work hard to develop the region and make sustainable use of the new economic opportunities that arise. Next year, a ministerial meeting will be held in the Arctic Council when the Presidency is transferred from Iceland to Russia. The Arctic Council turns 25 in 2021. I hope that next year's ministerial meeting will be well marked. Despite the fact that security policy is specifically excluded from the Arctic Council's mandate, as you know, cooperation helps to support the objective of low-voltage by providing the Arctic states with a common framework and a forum for dialogue and cooperation • and this is so much needed. I would like to stress that we are working within the Kingdom Community and the government to strengthen the efforts in the Arctic, which is very important to us. I look forward to the governments of Denmark, Greenland and the Faroe Islands in the coming months putting a final hand in the work on the new Arctic strategy, not least in dialogue with our parliaments. That is a very important part of it. Then there are several rapporteurs, and I would like to comment on that which has raised various issues in the debate. First, to sir. Christoffer Aagaard Melson in relation to the development of business in a sustainable way.• The climate and environment were also mentioned by the Left as an important, important priority: I fully agree. In relation to the discussion, which is also a debate, I believe that Mr. Søren Espersen mentioned it, i.e. whether to choose between Denmark and the United States.I think we have a kingdom community that is unique in the world. By the way, honorary consuls are something you have for a number of countries. I do not have the list here, but at least I know that in Greenland it is about the Czech Republic, Benelux, Germany, Finland and Norway. Iceland has a regular consulate, and the United States has opened a consulate. So there are plenty of countries that have either honorary consuls, who are not sent diplomats, but are some who have among other things. the role of being able to issue a passport if you lose it etc. or have other functions. There has also been an honorary consul from Russia in the Faroe Islands for a number of years, so that is perfectly normal. It was also asked whether we could then ask for one, for example. in Murmansk. On the Danish side, we also have honorary consuls elsewhere in Russia. You can do that. There is such a principle of reciprocity. If we say yes to the appointment of an honorary consul, then we also expect the country to be allowed to give us permission if we ask for the opposite. So it's perfectly normal. Now it's probably hard to find someone in Murmansk who wants to be Danish honorary consul, so at the moment we use the Norwegian. Norway has a regular consulate, and then we use it. So that way we can also help each other. And I'm looking at sir. Bertel Haarder, because we know that Nordic co-operation is also important. So it's just to say that this is a perfectly normal practice, and there may be consular cases where it's an advantage to have an honorary consul somewhere in the world. We know about this too from the Danish side. So I think it might be demystified that way. The report states that the purpose of this mechanism is to contribute to a common situational picture and to provide an opportunity to coordinate positions on, for example: international incidents affecting the safety of the Nordic region. ParlaMint-DK_20201020180724	Thank you for that and thank you for your many good kicks, remarks, questions and, not least, interest. It challenges security and stability and thus also the free and democratic foundation on which Denmark and the other Nordic countries are founded. We agree on that in the North we must stand together to deal with it. We were not far into the Danish NORDEFCO Presidency until I, together with the other Nordic countries, took up our new so-called crisis consultation mechanism. It was not just an exercise that we had practiced until the turn of the year, but because we were faced with a crisis that had to be dealt with: the attack on Al Asad Airbase. And then the corona knocked on the door, and we used the crisis consultation mechanism and coordinated across countries. It is clear that when we can put ourselves together in this way and exchange information in a special network that we only share in NORDEFCO, it is because we trust each other. It is not just something that has suddenly arisen; it is something we have spent a long time building. In this context, I would like to give a concrete example of trust. This summer there was a bomb-threatened passenger plane on its way from England to Oslo Airport, and if Norway had not shown such confidence, it would not have been Danish fighters who were asked to follow the plane all the way through Norwegian airspace and to land it. The fact that they saw breaking the sound barrier, when they quickly sat on their wings and flew up through Jutland, was of course something that attracted some attention. We deal with this noise in another direction, but I think it underlines how important it is that we have confidence and how important it is that we have close contact with each other and can work together. Nordic trust does not come on its own, and we must take care of it so that it stays there. It comes from the fact that over the years we in the Nordic countries have had and have a close community based on the same values, and also that we see the world with similar spectacles when it comes to the security policy situation. We are all small nations which, despite different alliances, face the same challenges. Together we take responsibility for our security in the Nordic countries. Cybersecurity is a very complex area that makes good sense to work together, not least because it requires very high expertise, which is not much in the respective countries. During the Presidency, a Danish-led working group has virtually put itself together to explore the possibilities for closer cooperation and exchange of experiences with education and training initiatives in the field of cybersecurity. We have different approaches in the Nordic countries, but we can clearly learn from each other how to maintain and develop competences in this area and also exchange information, which is the prerequisite for us to be able to deal with and warn about the challenges that exist. I also believe that, in the long run, when new technologies come in, we can, in cooperation, support it and share useful knowledge. Security in the Arctic is an area that requires greater and greater attention in Denmark and in general in the Nordic countries, and that is why, during the Presidency, we have worked to strengthen the defence and military dialogue on knowledge sharing between the Nordic countries on the Arctic. And precisely security in the Arctic is the issue that I put at the top of the agenda when I meet with my Nordic colleagues in November. I think it is obvious to explore ways of cooperating more closely in this area. There is no doubt that the United States plays an absolutely crucial role in the security of the North and Europe, and that is why it has also been important to push on to strengthen the Nordic transatlantic relationship work. In this context, and in concrete terms, the participation of the United States in the great Nordic flight exercise Arctic Challenge Exercise is absolutely crucial. Therefore, during the year of the Presidency, Denmark, together with the other Nordic countries, has worked to ensure a solid American commitment to future exercises as well. Positive progress has been made in this direction. You should not be an expert in security policy to understand why it is important that we have close defence cooperation with our Nordic neighbours. In short, it's about geography. To put it at the forefront: if something happens in one country, it will affect the others. And that is why it is at the heart of Vision 2025 and an important priority in the year of the Presidency to strengthen the ability to cooperate in crises and conflicts. Cooperation in NORDEFCO is already strong, but has so far limited itself to cooperation in peacetime. During the Danish Presidency, therefore, we have also focused on strengthening the ability to cooperate during crises and conflicts. And it is clear that in particular the crisis consultation mechanism, which in Swedish is called criskonsultationmekanism, sounds a little more schwung-like, has been a crucial tool for this. And there was also a major crisis rolling in with corona, where we have actually had great, great use of the mechanism and gained some useful experience. Finally, we have focused on strengthening Nordic co-operation in the material field, among others. in relation to cooperation with the Nordic defence industry and the European Defence Fund. Nordic co-operation is characterised by strong cohesion and resilience, which has clearly marked the year of the Presidency, where we have carried out a series of activities and meetings throughout the year. The cooperation has taken a good turn during the Nordic NORDEFCO Presidency, and when Denmark transfers the Presidency to Finland in 2021, we will of course continue our active commitment to developing the cooperation. I would also like to mention briefly our cooperation with the Nordic countries on international tasks, for example. within the framework of NATO or UN missions. Nordic makes sense to work together on international operational tasks. This means that together we can either solve larger tasks or send out contributions over a longer period of time. Let me give you a concrete example: the cooperation in the deployment of transport aircraft to the UN mission in Mali, to which several of the Nordic countries have also contributed since 2016. With regard to the Nordic emergency cooperation, in 2020 there has also been a positive development of cooperation, which helps to strengthen the country's resilience to natural and man-made disasters, and here too we have moved on to cooperation. With the so-called Copenhagen Declaration of February 2020, the Nordic Emergency Response Authorities showed how this should be done. It is about strengthened Nordic emergency cooperation in the light of climate change and more extreme weather conditions. With the declaration, the Nordic countries have given each other their hands on, among other things. working for more compatible equipment and sharing tactics and procedures, and we will also systematically share knowledge about natural hazards, develop common procedures for supporting each other and explore the possibility of joint exercises. It is essential that cooperation works in practice, and it does. To put it a little popular: The snakes fit together when it comes to it. At the same time, however, we must also think new things. ParlaMint-DK_20201020183404	Thank you, Madam President. Corona has changed our everyday life in so many ways. When we can't meet physically, we meet online instead. We must be better able to secure ourselves, but part of the responsibility also lies with the Internet-based communications services, also known as NUIK service providers. The bill we have to deal with today is precisely targeted at those providers. To put it quite simply, the rules governing traditional telecommunications services must also apply to the new Internet-based providers. That is why we need to implement the EU's telecommunications code for providers. We need to do this because we need to have confidence in the security of the platform where we communicate and where communication is taking place. We need to know who is listening, who is watching, and we need to know how the data is being processed. Internet-based services have come to stay and therefore we must ensure that they are subject to relevant information requirements and a notification obligation, just as the existing services are today. And we can do this by implementing the EU's telecommunications code. In the Social Democratic Party we uphold Danish security, both publicly and online, and therefore the Social Democratic Party supports the bill. And I had to say from Radicals that they are also in favour of the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020185202	Thank you for that, President. L 11, the bill, which we are going to deal with here, gives the Danish Business Authority a little more muscle to supervise the companies covered by dual-use export controls and certain sanctions against third countries. In concrete terms, it is proposed that the Danish Business Authority be given better opportunities to obtain relevant information from the companies, while at the same time the Danish Business Authority is given the opportunity to carry out inspections, both notified and unannounced. This should not, of course, be a nuisance to economic operators. Therefore, verification visits will take place on the basis of a risk assessment and thematic approach. In this way, we ensure that Danish companies that do not meet their social responsibilities can be controlled if necessary. The consultation answers show that several professional organisations are critical of the Danish Business Administration's ability to carry out unanticipated inspections without judicial orders. In the Social Democratic Party, however, we believe that the specific powers proposed for the Danish Business Authority are proportional. There are some specific ethical obligations for companies acting in the framework of dual-use export controls and sanctions against third countries. When dual use products that can be used for both military and civilian purposes are to be exported, there must be control of where they go and this entails special responsibility both for the specific companies and for the controlling authority. We should take that responsibility on board, and that is what we are doing with this bill, and that is why the Social Democratic Party supports the bill.

It will not be with conservative votes, because we are in complete agreement that fraud must be combated and that the Danish Business Authority must, of course, have the opportunity to control, especially with regard to industries that produce and export products that can be used as weapons, which, of course, the business community also supports in their consultation response. Verification visits can, of course, help optimize companies' efforts to comply with a quite complex set of rules, which is probably also the case in many cases. We cannot in any way support actions that are contrary to the Constitution, which give the right to untimely inspections without a warrant; that is what the Institute for Human Rights, Danish Lawyers, the Lawyers' Society and Justitia are responding to, of course, at the Justitia, which, incidentally, recently held a conference in this House with a focus on business rights. For it is of course violent when the authorities come on unannounced visits and may require full access to your business. That is why it must only happen if the problem is so great that it cannot be solved by other means. First of all, it raises the question of how big the problem is. The Minister justifies the bill by stating that in recent years there have been cases of political and media attention to Danish companies' compliance with the rules. It is not clear how many cases have been involved, but judging from the input that has come from the business community, it is a small handful of cases where there has even been no evidence that the company has deliberately infringed the rules. I hope that this proposal is not another in the series of laws that are only put into effect in order for the government to appear more capable of action. The second relevant question in this context is whether unanticipated verification visits make sense in this specific area. That is to say, will the companies be able to hide something from the authorities if they were notified of the verification visit? After all, the purpose of the verification visit is to verify whether the necessary authorisations have been obtained for the goods which a company has the Word does not have a here-and-now activity that can be taken into account. Verification visits also focus on checking the company's commercial documents, customer records, inventories, etc., as stated in the comments to the bill, i.e. records and data which cannot be disposed of or modified with a snap just if given notice of an inspection. The third relevant question is whether control is lacking today. The customs authorities can now check the specific goods exported by companies and the police can carry out searches at companies where there is a suspicion of breaches of the rules. The latter must be made through a warrant. When the authorities are given the opportunity, with this proposal, to carry out alerted inspections, it is difficult to see why they also need to be able to carry out unannounced inspections without this court order. And it is particularly surprising when it is also apparent from the bill that if there is a reasonable suspicion of a criminal offence, the Danish Business Authority will not be able to use the facility for inspections without prior warning. In such a case, the case will have to be handed over to the police. In other words, the Business Administration will be given more intrusive access to the companies than the one that the police have but in relation to matters of a less serious nature than those where the police already have to intervene today. I don't think that's connected. The Conservative People's Party therefore wants, first of all, to get the part about the untimely visits, that is, the part about the untimely visits without a warrant, out of the bill, otherwise we will not be able to see ourselves in this bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020191759	Thank you. No one has been inscribed to the remarks made to Mr Seligman. Henrik Dahl. Then it's the Minister of Business. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20201020191809	Thank you. And thank you for the interest in the proposal and for the good debate in this Chamber, and also for the many good and relevant consultation answers that have entered the process. Let me try to start by clarifying the background to the draft law before I turn to the key points of the draft law. It is really important for the government that Danish companies do not export products or carry out other activities that are contrary to international rules. Especially because we are talking about products that can, in the worst case, be used for war crimes, development of weapons of mass destruction and violations of human rights. It can, for example, be done. be the export of equipment that can be used militarily and exacerbate humanitarian crises, as in Syria and Yemen. It may also include the export of equipment that can monitor a population's telecommunications and violate citizens' fundamental human rights. The scope for control in this area is now relatively limited, and there are, for example, a number of problems. it is not possible to carry out verification visits at the premises of the companies in order to ensure that the companies actually comply with the rules in this area. These are the challenges we are trying to meet through a change in this law of authority. The aim is to strengthen the control and supervision of companies that export these so-called dual-use products, i.e. products that can be used civilly, but also militaryly or which act in areas covered by EU sanctions. With the bill, we specifically propose that the Danish Business Authority be given the power to carry out notified as well as unannounced inspections at Danish companies with dual-use export activities, or which trade with third countries or territories subject to international sanctions. We also propose that the Danish Business Authority be given the opportunity to require all relevant information for the purposes of the control provided by the companies concerned. These changes will allow more effective control of Denmark's international obligations and strength, that companies also comply with the basic social contract, which is so important for Danish society. In addition to the fact that the bill will ensure that companies that do not comply with the rules will be held accountable, the draft law also provides the Danish Business Authority with better opportunities to provide companies with targeted guidance, because of course it also needs to do so when you want a better regulatory compliance. We are aware that verification visits are an intrusive approach to Danish companies, and we therefore also propose that the individual verification visits should be given prior notice to the company. It will therefore be the starting point that there will be forewarned inspections. For us, however, it is also important that the board can also obtain access to a company unannounced if the specific circumstances so require. This is true in situations where control will be in vain if the visit has been announced in advance. It can, for example, be done. be in situations where an undertaking refuses to participate in a notified inspection or where there is a strong suspicion that a company retains certain information for the Danish Business Administration. I would like to stress that the main objective of export controls and sanctions is that we do not contribute directly or indirectly to activities such as, among other things, agriculture. development of weapons of mass destruction, war crimes, violations of human rights or the like. This is obviously an important responsibility which Denmark cannot afford to neglect. Overall, this bill will improve the prevention and control of non-compliance with these rules. I would like to turn to some of the specific comments that have come first to my radical colleague Mrs Katrine Robsøe. I think it is obvious to follow up on what experience we are gathering with these new powers. So, what good or possibly bad experience is gained from carrying out the inspections that may appear? What do we learn from the use of counsel? Indeed, it may be that guidance efforts are in fact the really effective action that will enable us to achieve the objective of meeting our international commitments. So it should just be given as an undertaking that we will follow it up together. Several rapporteurs have then touched on the various responses to the hearings with critical voices, particularly in relation to the question of the untimely inspections. There has been some criticism of this and, as I said in my speech, we know that verification visits are an intrusive measure, and therefore, as a starting point, the inspections will also be announced, just as the selection of individual companies will of course be based on a sound professional risk assessment carried out by the Danish Business Authority. Inspections without prior warning will be carried out if special circumstances warrant it for example if an enterprise refuses access to the company in connection with a notified inspection or otherwise makes it difficult for a planned inspection to take place. Then concerns have also been expressed about the scope of information to which the Danish Business Authority can access. And there I just think it is important to remember that the intention is simply to enable the collection of relevant information and not to collect large amounts of material that have no relevance whatsoever to the Danish Business Authority's supervision. The information which can be immediately identified as relevant shall include, inter alia: commercial documents and records of customers and goods. Then the question of legal certainty has also been raised in the course of the debate. And there I would just like to say that compliance with the principles of the law on legal certainty, among other things. proportionality of the intervention and the right not to incriminate oneself is, of course, of central importance in the proposal, precisely in order not to harm the legal certainty of companies. The Business Administration, which will be responsible for these visits and supervision in this area, already has practical experience of similar supervisory powers in the field of money laundering, where inspections can also be carried out with and without warning. The approach to inspections that we are proposing here is therefore similar to that which the Danish Business Authority is currently conducting in the field of money laundering. So there is no provision for a control which is significantly different from the existing one in the field of money laundering. And in addition, I also feel the urge to mention this issue also because it was raised by the Conservative rapporteur, where it almost sounded as if Denmark went very, very far in relation to other countries • that I just think it is part of the debate that both Germany and Sweden have confirmed to the Danish Business Authority that they have a corresponding opportunity to go on inspections without warning. In this sense, therefore, there is no question of a single Danish side, although I think that during the course of the debate it sounded that way. So I think it should be my attempt to give the fallen remarks a few lines along the way. And, of course, I look forward to the committee reading, and we are always at the disposal of dialogue, which has always been wanted and, of course, will be very happy to respond to the questions that need to be clarified in the process.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020194154	Thank you for that, President. As the President mentioned, the bill here is about amending the auditor's law, and it is about changing the rules for the auditor's examination, tightening the requirements for auditors and implementing some EU rules. First of all, I would like to say that there is a desire in the industry to have more approved auditors trained, and precisely the examination form with the low pass rates has been mentioned as one of the barriers to attracting more students. In January of this year, the report presented proposals for a new Auditor's examination, and it is against this background that it is proposed to change the exam so that more will be able to pass without compromising the professional level. Secondly, the possibility of acting as an approved auditor is limited if a person is or has been subject to bankruptcy proceedings under the law of change. It is already a requirement to be approved as a state-approved accountant that one is not in the process of reconstruction or bankruptcy, and in future bankruptcy proceedings will therefore also be disqualifying. In concrete terms, it is proposed that approved auditors should be restricted in their professional activities, while for a number of years it will prevent persons from being approved as accountants if bankruptcy proceedings are imposed. Thirdly, it is proposed that an auditor should be able to refuse his approval, while a case for precisely refusing approval is pending in the Board of Auditors, and an auditor should not be able to continue his work without a position on the part of the Court of Justice, even if he has refused his approval of the Board of Auditors. The court has to decide on that. Fourthly, parts of the EU Auditor's Directive are implemented. The auditors can be regarded as representatives of public confidence, they bear a great responsibility for each of them to answer their taxes, that the money is right and that things are going well. It is an important and entrusted task that we as a society give to the auditors. The scandals we have seen in the banking world in recent years simply underline the importance of society having a competent and loyal body of trust representatives, which is why it is crucial that we can have confidence in the work of auditors, while at the same time avoiding the loss of accountants in the future. I would just like to say that I have a worthwhile hope that with this review we will also come up with a situation in which the first four pages of statements of account are not a write-off of responsibility, but it may well be that this is a discussion that we will have to discuss in committee and in debate with the Minister. However, we believe that the bill here will ensure the future of the approach to the auditor's profession, while at the same time giving us a better opportunity to crack down on the stumbled vessels, and that is what we in the Socialist Group think is sensible, and that is why we support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020194941	The aim of the proposed bill is to strengthen the requirements of an auditor as a public trust representative in companies and to change the rules for the auditor's examination of the public auditors, and then there is also a minor amendment to the statutory auditor's law in relation to the statutory auditor's directive from the European Union. I also know that the Minister has heard about this. It is true that today there are, in fact, some 4,500 people who have been sentenced to bankruptcy cartels, and of course there are also a great many who have really been in bad faith and who have gone in with care and willfully committed fraud and circumvention of rules, etc. But I just have to arrest the premise a little, because it is also in my view and in the view of the Danish People's Party that a great many of those who are going to have bankruptcy quarantines today have not actually acted in bad faith, but have actually done what they could to get the companies in question restored. For example, it is the case that you can be sentenced to bankruptcy if you enter as an investor in privately run companies, where it turns out that the company has for some time failed to settle VAT and a tax. ParlaMint-DK_20201020200111	Thank you, sir. Henrik Dahl. Then it's the Minister of Business.
ParlaMint-DK_20201020200120	Thank you. And thank you for the fallen and positive remarks and expressions. After all, the legislative proposal we are discussing here is intended to strengthen the requirements of auditors, such as what they are, namely the representatives of public confidence, and to change the rules governing the auditor's examination of state-authorized auditors. And I am pleased to note that the draft law has also received a very positive and fine reception overall during the public hearing, including by the professional organisations. It is, of course, the alpha and the omega that we can trust the auditors as representatives of public confidence. They must ensure and maintain confidence in the financial information on which our economy is based. It cannot therefore be right for an accountant, for example. may act as a representative of the public's confidence if he or she is or has been subject to bankruptcy proceedings where he or she has been assessed unfit to take part in the management of a business because of grossly indefensible business conduct. The draft law therefore limits the possibility of acting as an approved accountant and thus acting as a public trust representative if a person is or has been subject to bankruptcy proceedings under the law of change. Unfortunately, we have examples of auditors continuing their activities as an accountant in judicial proceedings against the Board of Auditors and, in some cases, even training the case before the courts. We must have stopped an accountant from working for a long time if the person in question has seriously violated the rules and the Board of Auditors has therefore withdrawn the auditor's approval. The statutory auditor's board will therefore have the opportunity, with the bill we are discussing here, to temporarily dismiss the statutory auditor's approval, while a disqualification case is pending at the board. At the same time, in the future, the court will have to decide whether an auditor who has been dismissed as an auditor of the Board of Auditors may continue to operate while the case is pending before the courts. In practice, this means that if you have been deprived of your auditor's approval, you cannot speculate about appealing and withdrawing the decision while acting as an approved auditor. When you are a public trust representative, you have very special tasks and have to have very specific competences at a high professional level, and therefore the new rules on the audit examination will help to ensure that the public can continue to have confidence that the presentation of statements by auditors reflects a high quality. In particular, the change consists of the change in the structure of the exams, with three written modules, i.e. samples, being replaced by one written sample, which is held over two days. At the same time, the emphasis is placed more on the auditor's core tasks, namely the submission of statements, for example. for the annual accounts of undertakings. That should actually be what I wanted to say about the fallen remarks. I would just like to make one comment on Hans Kristian Skibby's question about the accountability and audit issue. Unfortunately, this is still a topical political issue and the government intends to convene political negotiations, and we will convene them this year so that we can start a political process on it. But the reason why it is not part of the proposal here is not that there is still a political desire to do something about it. I think we share the ambition and therefore there will be political negotiations on that subject.

It turns out that one of the most important instruments in relation to the start of smoking by children and young people is price increases. The proposal we are discussing here is an agreement between the Government and the Left, Radical, SF, Enhedslisten, Alternative and then us Conservative. In short, it is a question of preventing children and young people from being positively affected by tobacco products and other nicotine products and electronic cigarettes; that children and young people do not smoke during school hours and on matricles that house educational establishments with pupils under the age of 18; that we ensure that tobacco products are not sold to young people under the age of 18; that the penalties for violation of the sales ban are tightened; and, finally, that it is easier for municipalities to provide smoking cessation products so that those who want to get out of their addiction can also be helped to do so. We are delighted with the overall proposal, although we will try to correct a few elements in the committee's work. For example, we agreed in the agreement that standardised packages should be introduced. We did so because we want cigarette advertising to be restricted so that fewer young people start smoking, but we can see that there are some tobacco producers who are now starting to put advertising content in the cigarette packets themselves and, at the same time, are also starting to put advertising on cigarette filters. Our starting point is that the packages must be neutral, both inside and outside. In addition, I am thinking of exceptions to the specialties for cigars, pipes and pipe tobacco, which have probably become equally narrow enough. It is also unclear whether the derogation for the adult product, namely cigars, applies to the whole cigar family or whether it is only some types. We look forward to this proposal helping to ensure that we have a non-smoking youth year, and I look forward to the work of the committee. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020213550	Thank you. It is always a little exciting to be allowed to draw in the big lottery of cases you can get to fill in on.
