The draft law, as everyone knows, translates into the political agreement on a plan of action against children and young people's smoking, which the government and a broad majority in the Danish Parliament agreed on last December. It is therefore a legislative proposal that brings about a very ambitious plan. We are launching a wide range of initiatives aimed at effectively preventing and curbing smoking and the use of other tobacco products and nicotine products among children and young people, and which patient associations and professionals have been in demand for several years. With the agreement, we do it all at once, and it helps to make Denmark a pioneering country in this area. Children and young people are particularly vulnerable to the harmful effects of tobacco and other nicotine products. At the same time, most people start smoking before they reach the age of 18, thus creating at an early age the nicotine addiction that can harm them for the rest of their lives. The figures show that there are grounds for action. Every day 40 children and young people start smoking. Smoking is the biggest single cause of disease and death in Denmark and the biggest threat to public health. Smoking is very unevenly distributed and thus also the largest single factor that creates inequality in health in Denmark. Among the concrete measures in the bill are proposals that tobacco should be stored away in shops so that it does not tempt children and young people and that standardized packaging is introduced for all tobacco products and e-cigarettes so that they are given a uniform expression. Smoke-free school time is introduced in primary, secondary, boarding and upper secondary education. More stringent penalties are laid down for selling tobacco to children and young people under the age of 18 or for violating the rules of the Smoking Act. The rules on the prohibition of advertising, sponsorship and distribution are further strengthened. It also lays down a ban on the sale of e-cigarettes and tobacco products other than cigarettes. fruit and sweets tasting to prevent those products becoming a road to nicotine addiction and ordinary cigarettes for young people. We knew that when such an ambitious and far-reaching plan is implemented, there is a risk that the European Commission or other Member States will have objections, and the Commission has this to the ban on tasty tobacco products. For us, it does not seem logical for health reasons if we are not to ban flavours in tobacco products other than cigarettes and rolling tobacco, where they are already banned. The government is therefore proposing to retain the provisions of the bill, but we must wait until EU law is in place for the provision to enter into force. We have replied to the Commission and are now waiting for their reaction. We must respect the European Union, but we must also challenge them when EU rules end up standing in the way of public health. The government will continue to do so. If necessary, we will also address this in the context of the review of the Tobacco Products Directive. The measures implemented by the legislative proposal are, overall, a huge step forward for public health, and it is essential that we reverse developments. With all these measures, Denmark becomes one of the countries in the world that puts the most comprehensive and collected against children and young people's smoking. I hope that the draft law will receive a good committee reading, in which the Minister for Health and the Elderly will of course answer any questions that the committee may have on the way.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020215709	Thank you. As you know, today we are dealing with L 62, which deals with the scientific treatment of clinical testing of medical and non-medical equipment. The draft law is based on amendments to the EU regulations in force in this area. A rather central part of the bill is about moving the scientific assessment of clinical trials from regional scientific committees to new state specialised medical committees. We welcome this in the Enhedslisten. Although we are often concerned about centralisation, we are dealing with a highly specialised area where it is crucial that we bring together the most important competences, so that assessments always take all relevant considerations into account and protect citizens and patients from faulty or risky equipment. It also follows from the relevant EU legislation that the Danish rules need to be adjusted so that it should no longer be a requirement that importers and distributors of in vitro diagnostic medical devices and devices should keep their invoices for 5 and 15 years depending on the equipment. The purpose of the storage has been to enable the Ministry to use invoices as part of the market surveillance of the area. Now, the demand is being replaced by the requirement of the freedom of method for importers and distributors to inform their recipients and suppliers. It is important for Enhedslisten that it does not lead to a more indistinct picture of the equipment's movement through the supply chains. The Unity List also welcomes the fact that non-medical devices should now be subject to the same standard of scientific ethics as medical devices. There are several scare examples of the use of intrusive cosmetic and non-medical equipment that do not meet the normal safety requirements that we associate with and should expect from medical devices. We also support the fact that the government is proposing the continuation of security measures which are not harmonised through the regulations. This applies, for example, to the reporting of incidents and rules that medical devices can only be supplied in pharmacies. The bill also translates the recommendations in the Danish Medicines Agency's report •Evaluation of the 2018 association reform, which regulates the relationship between healthcare staff and the medical industry. The unit list has always fought for full transparency about the fitness of healthcare professionals and their relationship with industry when participating in equipment tests. Many applications will be major applications from industry, and it is crucial that it is professionalism and not industry's interests that weigh most when we authorise new equipment. It is essential for Enhedslisten that all those who are to participate in tests and tests in connection with the approval of new equipment are fully informed and have access to information about the trials they are involved in. That is why we are also pleased that, in the light of the criticisms of the hearings, the Minister has chosen to remove the restriction on the rights of data subjects from the draft law. At the same time, we, like the Children's Council, attach great importance to the need for minors between 5 and 15 years of age to be consulted and involved at all times in a clinical trial. That is why Enhedslisten can support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20201020220244	So it's exciting to see if the Minister can come after this little speech. Here you go, Minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20201020220307	Thank you for that, and I can't. But I think it's exciting to be a substitute. Many things are becoming wiser, including health, but we have come a long way this evening, and it is a great pleasure. If I can make it as short as the others, I won't be able to say. A full composition of a speech has been written, so I will work through it for all of you. I should like to begin by thanking you for your comments and for the largely good reception of the bill. In fact, I think I will twit it up and say that the bill is completely welcome. The reason why the government is making this proposal is that the first part of the new EU rules in the field of medical devices will apply from May next year. The new rules mean an improvement in the area and a boost in patient safety throughout the chain, which has been necessary. The government welcomes this. It is absolutely crucial that patients should be safe in that the equipment used in their treatment is safe and we must ensure that patients can be confident that the healthcare professionals responsible for the treatment are capable and open to their possible cooperation with the industry. In addition, we must safeguard Denmark's position of strength in Europe in the field of health research. This presupposes, among other things, that: the effective processing of applications for clinical trials of medical devices and products without a medical purpose. Therefore, a new regulatory setup is proposed for the scientific treatment of applications for clinical trials of medical devices and products without a medical purpose. The new EU rules give us reason to look at the existing national rules on medical devices, i.e. whether they are sufficient to fulfil the obligations laid down in the new EU rules. First of all, the rules of the Medical Devices Act must be adapted. On the one hand, there should be no national rules contrary to the new EU rules when they enter into force, and on the other hand, national rules should be laid down to allow the application of EU rules. This requires regulations on certain points. These include, as has been pointed out, language requirements for example. instructions for use, rules on control by the Danish Medicines Agency in this area and penalties for infringements of EU rules. In order to further strengthen the safety of patients, subjects and users of products without a medical purpose, the draft law also contains national special rules in some of the areas where it is permitted by the Medical Devices Regulation. First of all, the rules require the Danish Medicines Agency to approve clinical trials on humans of low- or intermediate-risk devices and of products with no medical purpose, and secondly, the requirement that distributors of products with no medical purpose be registered with the Danish Medicines Agency. And, as a final element, it is also proposed to lay down rules in the field of medical devices that are not harmonised by EU rules. It is against this background that we propose that a number of applicable provisions of the Medical Devices Act be continued, including the provisions on the possibility of rules on reporting incidents with medical devices and rules on the availability of medical devices only at the pharmacy. At the same time, the proposal provides that existing rules may be extended to products with no medical purpose in order to support the intention of EU rules to strengthen the safety of users of these products. That is, for example, the case. relevant with regard to the rules on the association of healthcare professionals with industry, where it is proposed to include companies working with non-medical devices. At the same time, the draft law implements the recommendations in the Danish Medicines Agency's report, which is called "Evaluation of the Association Reform" which appeared in December 2018. That evaluation shows that the eligibility rules are working well. These rules help to promote the professional benefit of cooperation between health professionals and industry and to ensure confidence in the viability of health professionals. There is only a need for minor adjustments, such as: The aim is to extend the definition of companies covered by the associated rules to representatives in Denmark of foreign pharmaceutical companies and to introduce stricter rules on the association of doctors with specialist stores selling medical devices. Finally, the regulation on medical devices lays down stricter requirements for procedures and time limits for the assessment of applications for clinical trials of medical devices and products without a medical purpose. Denmark's compliance with these deadlines and procedures requires close cooperation between the Danish Medicines Agency and the committee system, and therefore a new main law proposes a new regulatory setup for the scientific treatment of applications for clinical trials of medical devices and products without a medical purpose. The examination of applications for clinical trials is thus proposed to be moved from the regional scientific committees to new state specialised medical committees. This will provide a good basis for effective processing of applications for clinical trials of medical devices, with which Denmark will be able to maintain its position of strength in Europe in the field of health research. Good health research framework is crucial for companies' ability to develop new, effective and safe medical devices and is the foundation for patients to have access to better medical devices. Then I should just like to respond briefly to a few comments that fell along the way. I think Mrs Liselott Blixt asked about the choice of language in relation to instructions for use and the new option. As a starting point, it will be Danish, but with exceptions. I do not know whether we are getting the Danish People's Party enthusiastically excited about the European Union today, but there is at least an opportunity here that one can choose to say that it should be in Danish as a starting point. Then, along the way, the question of the so-called recycling of one-off equipment was also raised. It has been there before, I know, but I think there is good reason to repeat it. It is, of course, a crucial premise that patients can safely be treated with medical devices when they are in our healthcare system, and there is no intention to change that with the bill here. There will be strict restrictions on when the recycling of medical devices can take place, and the Danish Medicines Agency's professionals, in cooperation with the Statens Serum Institute, will assess whether a specific type of equipment should be recyclable. Then a proper announcement will have to be made about it. So the point is that the equipment has to be as safe and it has to be the same as when it is first used. Then I don't think I can come up with anything else about it.

It is Marcus Aurelius, so I will not quote him, but just note that no more have asked for the floor, and that is the end of the debate.
